Le Journal

The notebook, day by day

The development log, set as an exhibition catalogue: findings, the timeline of each day, the commits, every Dhrystone run, a second measure from a scaled 7-Zip kernel and a third from a JavaScript interpreter. Text follows the DEVLOG.md in the repository, which remains the full record. The small tag on each entry names where its time came from.

Instructions, per beat of the clock

Best Dhrystone result per version with the reference runtime, in simulation (ink line, gold dots; each dot opens its portrait). The thin sage line is the fast runtime where it was run. 72 logged runs. No FPGA hardware was measured.

Le Second Étalon

A second measure beside Dhrystone: the LZMA benchmark kernel of 7-Zip, scaled down to run in the simulator, in instructions per clock (MIPS per MHz) for compression and decompression. Every row is a simulation of that scaled kernel except the Cortex-X925 row, a native run of the same kernel on a laptop core. None of these is a 7-Zip benchmark score or a hardware result for this processor. The goal is about 10.9 / 3.35 on the scaled kernel, derived from a 10,000 MIPS single-thread 7-Zip rating at 4 GHz; the same goal on the full-size (d22) kernel is about 2.03 / 2.18. The last three rows are that full-size kernel, a different, larger workload whose bars do not compare with the rows above; in the deciding configuration (slow loads, a large and distant memory) version 0.15.4 with one miss at a time reaches 1.53 / 1.98 in simulation (75% / 91%), and version 0.16.0 with four misses in flight reaches 2.19 / 2.32 (108% / 107%): the goal is met in point estimate, though the decompress interval reaches below it.

compress decompress goal (outlined)

  1. Goal, scaled kernel about; derived from 10,000 MIPS in 7-Zip at 4 GHz

    10.90 MIPS/MHz compress
    3.35 MIPS/MHz decompress

  2. Cortex-X925, native laptop core running the same scaled kernel natively; not a simulation

    8.34 MIPS/MHz compress
    2.59 MIPS/MHz decompress

  3. 0.16.1 out of order, 64 KiB caches simulated, scaled kernel; 0.16.2 and 0.17.0 count within 0.02% of its clocks

    9.41 MIPS/MHz compress
    3.29 MIPS/MHz decompress

    2.89 MIPS/MHz decompress

    2.03 MIPS/MHz compress
    2.18 MIPS/MHz decompress

  9. 0.15.4 out of order, full-size (d22) kernel, deciding configuration simulated, sampled (30 pieces per phase, 95% CI ±10.9% / ±22.9%); loads at four clocks, a 16-beat recent-beat buffer, 4 MiB L2 at 20 clocks, memory 350 clocks away, one miss at a time; goal not met, 75% / 91%

    1.53 MIPS/MHz compress
    1.98 MIPS/MHz decompress

  10. 0.16.0 out of order, full-size (d22) kernel, deciding configuration, four misses in flight simulated, sampled (60 pieces per phase, 95% CI ±6.8% / ±10.2%, low ends 2.04 / 2.09); as above but N_MISS 4 and MEM_GAP 8; goal met in point estimate, 108% / 107%; the decompress interval reaches below the goal

    2.19 MIPS/MHz compress
    2.32 MIPS/MHz decompress

Scale: 0 to 12 MIPS/MHz, linear. Out-of-order rows under Verilator, whole program; multicycle and pipelined under Icarus.

L'Interprète

A third measure: JavaScript, run by the QuickJS-ng 0.18.0 bytecode interpreter (no JIT) through nine kernels: properties, a scheduler, a fluid stencil, SHA-256, JSON, regular expressions, strings, a DOM-like tree and closures. Each bar is the geometric mean over the nine of the laptop's clocks divided by ours for the same timed call, so 1.0 is the laptop's own rate per clock. The goal is 1.5x on every kernel and as the mean. The laptop row is a native measurement; every other row is a simulation of this processor, not a hardware result, and the flat-memory rows flatter it against the laptop's real caches and memory.

  1. Goal 1.5x the laptop per clock, on every kernel and as the geometric mean

    1.50x the laptop per clock

  2. Cortex-X925, native the laptop core running the same engine, harness and script; median of 16 runs, CPU cycle counter; not a simulation

    1.00x the laptop per clock

  3. 0.17.0 out of order, hard float, 64 KiB caches simulated, flat memory, 16 sampled pieces per kernel; no kernel reaches the goal; 56.3% of the goal

    0.844x the laptop per clock

  4. 0.17.0 out of order, soft float, 64 KiB caches simulated, as above with the FPU off and compiler-rt, 8 pieces per kernel; faster than hard float through instruction-cache layout; 61.1% of the goal

    0.916x the laptop per clock

  5. 0.17.0 out of order, hard float, 1 MiB instruction cache a probe, not a design: simulated, one piece per kernel, 500K warm-up; 76% of the goal

    1.15x the laptop per clock

  6. 0.17.0 out of order, hard float, 7-Zip's deciding configuration simulated, slow loads and a large distant memory, 4 pieces per kernel with wide intervals; the order of magnitude is the result; 18.9% of the goal

    0.284x the laptop per clock

Scale: 0 to 1.6x the laptop per clock, linear. Out-of-order rows under Verilator, sampled from rv_iss checkpoints with a 2M-instruction warm-up; output checked against the native run by hash.

21 novembre 202620 novembre 2026Les Essais

2026-11-21

21 novembre 2026

slot-3 branch trial, validation of 0.7.1, pipelining plan

Findings

  1. A third-slot conditional branch does not pay: it moves branches from the start of a group to the end of the previous one, but a branch at the start of a group already folds its target into the second slot, so the clock count barely changes (+0.4% DMIPS/MHz). Dropped.
  2. The independent review of 0.7.1 found no bug in the committed design but a missing test: allowing a multiply to pair (the pre-0.7.1 rule) puts a store on the bus early, and the regression did not notice. The test is now in the regression and catches it.
  3. Absolute performance, not DMIPS/MHz, decides the next steps: 0.3.0 reaches 22.49 MHz on ECP5 (about 21 DMIPS), with 79% of its critical path in routing. A pipelining plan was drafted (below); its figures are estimates until measured.

Timeline

  1. 00:00 to 00:02

    Candidate 0.7.2 written: conditional branch in the third slot (compare on the third slot's operands, target pc3 + imm, taken target misaligned does not issue there; the group's successor is the branch's outcome). New tests triple_branch, triple_branch_misaligned. Testbench tb_core.sv now counts second-slot, third-slot and third-slot-branch retirements on its DONE line, and test_cpu.py checks that dual_issue_fold and triple_issue use their slot (default caches, no wait cycles)

    output
  2. 00:02:25

    Regression: 52 programs x 3 wait patterns = 156 runs pass with default caches (15.7 s) and with 2-line caches (11.8 s); triple_branch retires 2 branches from the third slot

    output
  3. 00:04:31

    Unlogged Dhrystone run on the candidate (rv64imb, reference runtime, -NoDevlog): 2.2250 DMIPS/MHz, 255.8 clocks per run against 256.8 (-0.4%)

    output
  4. about 00:06

    Blocker count on the candidate's trace (a scratch script groups the measured profile trace into issue groups): pairs stopped by a third-slot branch fell from 36.0 to 3.0 per run, but pairs stopped by a third-slot store rose from 18.0 to 48.0; triples rose by only 1.0 per run. The branch used to start the next group and fold its target into the second slot; now that target starts a group of its own. Candidate reverted; tests kept as plain differential tests

    output
  5. 00:00 to 00:07

    Independent validation of 0.7.1 by a review agent in a separate worktree at c1b113f: regression 150/150 (14.1 s) and 150/150 with 2-line caches (12.3 s); check_bitmanip.py passes (6.3 s, 28 B instructions, 33,124 and 15,003 retired); visualizer lessons pass after a rebuild (15.9 s); check_repo.py passes; RTL hashes, cpu/VERSION, tag and the 0.7.1 run rows agree with the records. No bug found in the multiply path. Mutants: reverting the pairing rule (!m_op to !is_div) passed the whole regression; three other multiply mutants were caught. Its focused scratch test caught the first. Tools: Icarus Verilog 12.0, Python 3.12.3 (WSL) and 3.12.10 (Windows), clang 18.1.3 (WSL) and 23.1.5 (Windows)

    output
  6. 00:07

    Review follow-ups: new tests mul_then_store_pad0, mul_then_store_pad1 (a store after each multiply kind) and mul_into_x0; regression 55 programs x 3 = 165 runs pass with default and 2-line caches; with the pairing mutant applied, mul_then_store_pad1 fails at event 14 (a store's W line before the multiply's R line). runlog.py no longer strips the leading space of git status output, which had cut the first character from the first dirty file name in run logs (for example "EVLOG.md"). cpu/architecture.md now lists the DIVIDE and MULTIPLY states and excludes multiplies from first-slot pairing in its text. Also reported: cpu/programs-verification.json and cpu/visualizer-verification.json still hold the RTL hashes of the initial commit (the regenerated copies in the working tree are current), and cpu/dhrystone-rv64im-fastrt-results.json is still the 0.6.0 run

    output
  7. about 00:05

    Pipelining plan drafted by a planning agent from the RTL and the measurements: five short stages (PC, cache read, decode and register read, execute, memory and retire), instruction and data caches in block RAM, register file in LUT RAM (two copies for two read ports; a live-value table for two-wide), forwarding from two registered stages, static then BTB prediction, two-wide issue at most (slot 3 dropped), traps acted on at retirement. Estimates, not measurements: single-issue pipeline about 0.8 DMIPS/MHz at 60 to 75 MHz; two-wide about 1.3 DMIPS/MHz at 50 to 60 MHz (65 to 80 DMIPS, against about 21 for 0.3.0)

    output
  8. 00:07:44

    ECP5 synthesis of 0.7.0 and 0.7.1 still running after 30 minutes in Yosys synth_ecp5 (0.3.0 took 59.5 s)

    output
  9. 00:08:39

    Commit 15074b8: multiply-pairing and slot-use tests, slot-3 branch trial record, runlog.py fix

    git
  10. 00:09 to 00:13

    Pipeline what-if model sim/cpu/bench/pipeline_model.py (written by a side agent; Python 3.12.10) replayed the measured Dhrystone traces through an in-order pipeline with configurable prediction, width and stalls (instruction and data caches assumed to hit; redirect from E costs 3 bubbles, from D 2). Note: the rv64imb trace it read came from the reverted slot-3 branch candidate (255.8 clocks per run, against 256.8 for 0.7.1). rv64imb reference, clocks per run: single issue, not-taken 858.1 (0.663 DMIPS/MHz); static 813.1 (0.700); 64-entry BTB with a 4-entry return stack 639.3 (0.890); perfect prediction 579.1 (0.983); two-wide with a 64-entry BTB 465.2 (1.223); two-wide with perfect prediction 405.1 (1.405). Branch handling is the largest lever (about 279 of 858 clocks are control penalties without prediction); two-wide issue saves 145 to 175. Break-even: even two-wide with perfect prediction needs 1.58x the clocks of the measured core, single issue with a 64-entry BTB 2.50x, so a pipeline only wins if its clock is that much faster. Command: python -B sim/cpu/bench/pipeline_model.py plus sensitivity runs (BTB 32 to 256 entries, return stack 4 to 16 (no difference), tagless BTB, no late store data, fixed divide cost)

    output
  11. 00:12 to 00:19

    Branch pipeline created from 15074b8. Candidate 0.8.0 written: rv64_core.sv replaced by a four-stage single-issue pipeline (F: registered instruction-cache read with fill bypass; D: register read, JAL and backward branches redirect F (1 bubble); E: execute, forwarding from M's integer result and from the last retirement, mispredict redirect (2 bubbles); M: data cache read registered as the instruction enters M, bus access, retirement, traps). Load or multiply result used by the next instruction: 1 clock; store data from a load is taken in M without a stall. A store to the beat of a younger fetched instruction flushes and refetches; traps wait until no fetch is outstanding. Register file has no reset loop; a sequencer zeroes it in 32 clocks after reset. Decode, B, multiply and divide logic reused; ports and trace unchanged (retire2/retire3 stay 0). test_cpu.py slot-use checks emptied on this branch (single issue)

    output
  12. 00:17 to 00:19

    Pipeline candidate: compiles with iverilog -g2012 -Wall without warnings; regression 55 programs x 3 = 165 runs pass on the first full run with default caches and with 2-line caches; check_bitmanip.py passes. Unlogged Dhrystone (rv64imb, reference runtime): 0.7944 DMIPS/MHz, about 716 clocks per run (0.7.1: 2.2163). Better than the model's 0.700 for static prediction because this design's redirects cost 1 and 2 bubbles, not 2 and 3

    output
  13. 00:20:37

Regression 165/165 with default and 2-line caches. Unlogged Dhrystone (rv64imb, reference): 0.9216 DMIPS/MHz, about 618 clocks per run (+16% over 0.7944; model 0.890 with 3-bubble redirects)

Place and route running

  17. 00:26:26

Regression 165/165 (default and 2-line caches). Unlogged Dhrystone: 0.9216 DMIPS/MHz, unchanged: the remaining JALR misses are not return-stack timing (the model attributes them to BTB conflicts)

No faster than 0.3.0 (22.49 MHz): the critical path runs from M's exception flag through the stall logic and E's operand forwarding into the divider's set-up (operand magnitudes, leading-zero counts, early results) and on to M's result register

Regression 165/165 with default and 2-line caches; Dhrystone 0.9670 unchanged by the compression

  27. about 00:43

    ECP5 place and route of the pipeline with the divider set-up in M (p080c, 64-entry BTB, 512-line caches): 24.30 MHz (from 21.09), 25,039 cells, 456.5 s; critical path 41.2 ns (12.3 ns logic, 28.9 ns routing) from the data-cache tag block RAM through M's hit check, M's completion, E's and D's advance and the next-fetch-PC select into f_pc. The stall decision runs the length of the pipeline in one clock and its enables drive hundreds of flip-flops. Estimate: 0.3.0 21.3 DMIPS (22.49 MHz x 0.9473); this pipeline about 23 DMIPS (24.3 MHz x 0.9670 for the 256-entry BTB, whose own clock is not yet measured). Next: decouple fetch from the stall chain

    output
  28. 00:44 to 00:47

    Candidate 0.8.1, step 1 (rv64_pipe.sv): fetch decoupled from the stall chain. A 4-entry fetch queue sits between F and D (D takes F's instruction directly when the queue is empty); F advances whenever the queue is not full, a registered condition. E and D redirects are sent once, as soon as the stage knows, instead of when the stage advances (E waits for valid operands first). Store-to-fetched-beat and fence.i flushes are registered and act one clock after the retirement, discarding everything younger, including an instruction that entered M at that edge; the check also covers queue entries. The return stack moved to F (calls and returns update it as they are fetched). Regression (pipe core) 165/165 with default and 2-line caches; Dhrystone 0.9670, unchanged. A first attempt to start its ECP5 run died silently: a background job started from a WSL command that exited at once did not survive; later jobs wait a few seconds before the command exits

    output
  29. 00:49 to 00:53

    Step 2: X stage. M finishes its instruction in one clock without waiting: it retires it (integer result, load hit, multiply, or a bus access completed in that clock) or hands an unfinished bus access to X, which holds the identical request until the bus completes and then retires it; M waits while X is busy. So whether E may advance depends only on registers (X busy, divide still running), not on the data-cache hit; a bus error in M's first clock traps from X one clock later. Divides always take their set-up clock. X's data-cache write also updates the line M already read. Regression 165/165 (default and 2-line caches), B cross-check passes, Dhrystone 0.9670

    output
  30. about 00:55

    ECP5 result for the tagged 0.8.0 pipelined core (p080d, 512-line caches): 28.00 MHz, 27,495 cells, 348.1 s; critical path 35.7 ns (13.8 ns logic, 21.9 ns routing) from E's instruction through operand forwarding, the load/store misalignment check, E's exception flag and E's mispredict redirect into the next-fetch-PC select and the instruction-cache block-RAM address. About 27 DMIPS (28.0 x 0.9670) against about 21 for 0.3.0: the first absolute gain

    output
  31. 00:55 to 00:57

    Step 3: redirect checks split between D and E. D compares fetch's prediction with what it can compute itself (JAL target; conditional-branch target with the BTB's direction, or taken if backward without a BTB entry; fall-through for anything else; JALR keeps the prediction) and redirects once on a mismatch. E checks only a conditional branch's direction and a JALR's target, and no longer waits for its exception flag (a faulting instruction traps in M either way). Regression 165/165 (default and 2-line caches), B cross-check passes, Dhrystone 0.9670. ECP5 runs of steps 1, 2 and 3 (p081a, p081b, p081c) started

    output
  32. about 00:59

The path runs from the data-cache tag through M's hit check and "M needs the bus" into bus arbitration (a fetch waits for a data access), fetch completion, F's advance and the return-stack enables. Steps 2 and 3 keep that dependency

  37. 01:05

    New user direction: move toward the Apple M5 (an out-of-order design). Plan: a third implementation, rtl/cpu/rv64_ooo.sv, built in stages that each pass the full trace regression

    output
  38. 01:05 to 01:25

    Out-of-order model added to sim/cpu/bench/pipeline_model.py by a side agent (--ooo; Python 3.12.10; in-order output unchanged, checked with cmp). Caches always hit; ordering (a) loads wait for older stores to retire, (b) loads never wait (an upper bound), (c) loads wait for older stores to issue (store-queue forwarding). rv64imb reference trace, DMIPS/MHz: in-order w1 BTB256 0.961, w2 1.361; out-of-order 1-wide (a) 0.954 to 0.972; 2-wide, ROB 32, IQ 16, 2 ALUs, 1 memory port, checkpoint recovery: (a) 1.443, (c) 1.733, (b) 1.749; 4-wide ROB 64 3 ALUs (b) 2.428. Memory ordering is the largest lever ((a) to (c) +20%), then width; under (a) a load latency of 3 instead of 2 costs 15%; recovery (retire, early, checkpoint) is worth 3% here and 9% on the fast-runtime trace. Limits: no cache misses, no wrong-path occupancy, no clock-rate dimension

    output
  39. 01:06 to 01:22

    Stage A written (rtl/cpu/rv64_ooo.sv, 1,011 lines): the pipelined core's front end; rename of one instruction per clock onto 64 physical registers; 32-entry reorder buffer retiring one per clock; 8-entry issue queue with wakeup and select; an integer port (multiply/divide unit behind it) and a two-stage load/store port; stores write at retirement; loads issue when every older store has retired; mispredict redirects fetch at execute and rename waits for the branch to retire. Decode, B and M logic reused from the pipelined core. --core ooo added to test_cpu.py, check_bitmanip.py, run_bench.py, run_dhrystone.ps1 and the run log

    output
  40. 01:19 to 01:23

    First regression of stage A: 53 of 55 programs pass; memory_lanes and mul_then_store_pad0 fail at 19 wait cycles with "unstable stalled request". A debug bench (build/cpu/ooo-debug/) showed a load miss's held request dropped: completing a store in the load/store port's first stage also cleared its second stage. Fixed; that exposed hangs in three programs: when the first stage finished a store in the same clock the second finished a load, the second stage was not cleared, and the finished load later read an unwritten cache tag (X). Fixed by handling the two cases separately

    output
  41. 01:23 to 01:25

Unlogged Dhrystone (rv64imb, reference): 0.7888 DMIPS/MHz, below the pipelined core's 0.9670 and the model's 1-wide (a) 0.954: this design's load-to-dependent latency is 3 clocks and loads wait for older stores to retire

  43. 01:37:20

    git
  44. 01:37 to 01:40

    0.9.1 written (out-of-order core): a load issues once every older store has its address and data; in its second stage it scans the older stores still in the reorder buffer for its 8-byte beat and takes the youngest one's data when it covers every byte the load reads; on a partial overlap it waits for those stores to retire and reads the bus. A store retiring in the clock a load reads the cache passes its bytes into that read. Regression 165/165 with default and 2-line caches; B cross-check passes. Unlogged Dhrystone 0.9428 DMIPS/MHz (from 0.7888, +19.5%; the model predicted about +20% for this ordering change)

(2) Hang at 5,328 retirements: head load ld a3, 8(a1) at 0xdbc waiting, miss unit and bus idle, fb_lock=1 held by ROB 76, which had already retired, because its fill arrived after it finished (squash or completion before the fill). Fixed: the fill sets the lock only if the miss owner is still in flight (mu_owner_v, cleared when the owner is discarded or on a flush)

All fixed in 0.11.0 except the bench profile and the oldest-miss rule (open); the history row is corrected

Store bandwidth is not the limit; the change is not handed off. Real memory losses: the load at 0xa54 (read before a same-clock store address, then held by the load-wait table every run) and replays at 0x8cc

  128. about 12:28

  142. 12:44

    First periodic Codex review (build/cpu/ooo-debug/council/codex/review-20261121-1239.md, 319 s). Bugs: (1) the bus-candidate scan stops at rb_viol and rb_fencei but not at rb_vpbad, while retirement stops at rb_vpbad, so a store younger than a value-mispredicted load can be written and then discarded (assigned to memory, a likely cause of the fold_byte_loop failure); (2) a correct load can be marked as violating when an older store to its byte resolves after a younger store forwarded the right byte, setting a sticky untagged wait bit (memory, performance); (3) the middle engineer's probe counts 16 retirement lanes and a fixed IQ threshold, wrong for wider builds (middle). Suggestions: load lanes 4-5 and store-address 3 with the four-store path (the reference mix needs about 4.15 loads and 2.67 stores per clock at the goal; backend + memory); compare fetch/rename and retire widths separately (middle + frontend); a fourth branch-capable port (backend). All accepted for measurement

  208. 17:10 to 17:45

    Research agent on the LZMA compress bottleneck (read-only; RTL copies and probe benches in build/cpu/ooo-debug/council/lzma-research/, runs.log; commit bfde6eb RTL; Icarus 12.0, Verilator 5.020, Python 3.12.3; the probe benches do not check program results, every variant retired the base instruction count). The benchmark uses the SoC defaults (rv64_bram_soc.sv:8-9): 4 KiB direct-mapped I-cache, 4 KiB 2-way D-cache, 8-byte lines, one bus at 2 clocks per read, one miss at a time for the oldest load only. Measured on the whole compress kernel (Verilator, 1,067,885 clocks): the bus spends 59.5% of clocks on instruction fetch (317,735 I-cache misses, 104 MPKI); load misses 9.8% of clocks (17 MPKI); stores 12.7%; zero-retire clocks 41.9%; fetch queue empty 46.6%; ROB average 58 of 512 (full 0.2%); IQ full 10.5%; mispredicts 10.5 MPKI. Dataflow bound with perfect prediction and 1-clock loads: 18.8 IPC on piece 4, so dependences are not the limit. Pieces 0 and 2 touch 3.5 KB of code (fits the I-cache, 24 MPKI); pieces 4 and 12 touch 7.6-8 KB (105-112 MPKI). Decompress is bound by the range decoder's multiply chain (dataflow bound 3.74 IPC at 3-clock multiply) and by 29.4 MPKI of data-dependent bits no predictor learns. Variants (compress / decompress MIPS/MHz): I-cache 32 KiB direct-mapped 4.54 / 2.81 (GetMatches aliases CodeOneBlock at 32 KiB); I-cache 64 KiB 6.45 / 2.81; I 64 + D 32 KiB 7.38 / 2.89; I and D 128 KiB 7.48 / 2.89; 1-clock reads with 4 KiB caches 4.83 / 2.73; 2-clock multiply 3.42 / 2.79; 2-clock multiply with 128 KiB caches 7.68 / 3.24; IQ 256 no gain; letting any load start a miss hung both kernels (300 s timeout). Trace simulation: 16 KiB 2-way I-cache 1.6 MPKI, 32 KiB 2-way 0.6. Ranked proposals: (1) 2-way 16-32 KiB I-cache with multi-beat lines; (2) pipelined or burst bus reads (SoC contract change); (3) 32-64 B D-cache lines with burst fill; (4) multiply latency 3 to 2; (5) global-history direction predictor (trace estimate compress 12.1 to 7.2 MPKI); (6) several misses in flight (naive version livelocks). Flag from the agent: raising the SoC default cache size alone changes the measurement configuration, not the core; sizing the cache to this benchmark's code would be tuning.

Conclusion: at realistic latency, overlapping misses is worth about 40% of compress clocks, and stores must not wait behind a held load miss.

High: a branch to PC+4 corrupts the direction history (rename skips repair because both directions have the same next PC; execute infers the direction from the next PC); the measured gain and test passes are on 0.14.11, not 0.14.13, and the MPKI omits conditional redirects at rename (397 in compression); for timing, one fetch group can make 32 history-dependent predictions (128 tagged-table probes) and retirement up to 32 lookups and updates in a clock, beyond real predictors. Medium: retirement re-looks up the provider instead of using the one that predicted; TAGE trains even when a confident loop prediction bypassed it; all 4,096 useful entries are halved in one clock; storage is 8 KiB of tables plus 9 KiB of snapshots (about 17 KiB), not 7.5 KB.     Codex second opinion on memory hand-off 2 (several misses in flight; build/cpu/ooo-debug/coord/h2review/review.md): hold. Critical: a permanently faulting beat can livelock the oldest load when a younger load replays against the same beat on alternate clocks (its error is dropped, the tag re-allocated, and the duplicate-beat check vetoes entry 0), so the reserved entry does not guarantee progress; the engineer's fault test used different beats. High: the SoC's split mode breaks the multicycle and pipelined cores at N_MISS > 1; N_MISS 1 is not identical to the released 0.15.x (the hand-off is rebased on 0.14.13, and posted stores that miss the L2 now start a DRAM fill); a terminal trap can be taken with a split read outstanding; instruction fetch still uses tag 0 and blocks. Medium: no fairness in response arbitration; testbench gaps (tag not compared under backpressure, reset leaves queued responses, ambiguous read-write-response data rule); timing optimism (no DRAM start spacing at MEM_GAP 0, free same-line merges, no bank arbitration). All accepted; back to the memory engineer (codex/DECISIONS.md).

  294. 22:05

    Codex design review of diamond predication (build/cpu/ooo-debug/coord/predreview/review.md): hold the RTL. The oracle numbers reproduce from the saved results, but: (critical) the shape detector admits j X with a misaligned X that must trap at the jump PC; the taken arm's rename needs an evolving map, value-prediction replay of the selects, no folding or fusion across arms; conditional stores need a byte-level replay rule; (high) the exact trace per direction and one predictor-history convention must be specified; the bound charges none of the costs of fetching both arms (+12.9% instructions), ROB / IQ occupancy, select issue and latency; (medium) a hand-excluded PC lets the fallback learn the benchmark. Decision: design revision and a cost-charged cycle model first; a second review before any RTL.

  300. 22:40

    Middle: why a 256-entry IQ was slower (BOARD 22:33): not select priority but ALU-pair fusion, which pairs a producer with its reader only when both are renamed in the same clock; a bigger IQ stalls rename less, so more pairs split across rename groups. All of the +3.5K clocks are in the decompress output-CRC loop; with fusion off IQ 128 and 256 are equal. Proposed fix (keep the pending fusion producer across rename clocks): on 0.15.7 d12 386,369 / 133,976 to 384,720 / 133,233 (-0.43% / -0.55%); IQ 256 no longer slower. Coordinator: hand-off only if the hardware is plausible (a held producer with the clock charged), otherwise record the finding as "fusion is group-bound".

  301. 22:49

    Backend corrected d18 profile (probe v2: each zero-retire clock charged to the head's state at the clock's start; same 40 pieces and totals: released 0.15.7 52,763,111 / 9,131,347, h157 44,153,374 / 8,218,500). Compress zero-retire shares, released (59.5% zero-retire) / h157 N_MISS 4 (48.3%): head load on an L2 hit (12-99-clock episodes) 46.6% / 50.5%; head store waiting with the bus held by a data read 24.3% / 0.0%; load answered, waiting for its LD_LAT / L0 wake 13.8% / 20.7%; dependences 13.2% / 21.5%; short load waits 0.6% / 4.8%; ROB empty after recovery 0.1%; DRAM 0 (d18 fits the 4 MiB L2). ROB full 10.7% / 3.3% of clocks; 0.95 / 1.99 misses outstanding while the head waits. Decompress released / h157: dependences 41.0% / 53.9%, L2 25.0% / 15.1%, store-at-bus 16.5% / 0%, wake 13.3% / 16.1%, DRAM 0.1% / 8.9%. Compress at d18 is bound by L2 hit latency on the BT4 chain; on the blocking bus also by stores behind held reads, which several misses in flight removes. Bounds running (L2 12, MEM 200, ROB 1024, store buffer 16 on the release; N_MISS 8 on h157).

  310. 23:42

    SIMD study correction (BOARD 00:22; model bounds): the 00:18 numbers assumed 64 B L1 lines, but the core's L1 D lines are 8 B with per-beat miss entries and one allocation per clock. With the core's rules: the accepted build (VLEN = DLEN = 256, 2 FMA pipes) at N_MISS 8 gives P 17.35 (5% under the 18.2 goal) and S 9.70 (goal 9.0) with a 12-clock L2, but P 16.3 and S 6.7 with the deciding configuration's 20-clock L2; at N_MISS 4 P 15.5 and S 5.4. DLEN 512 / 2 FMA at N_MISS 8: P 23.2 (21.1 at L2 20), S at vl 32 11.4. 64 B L1 lines with whole-line fill (memory's deferred item) would add 25% to S (12.1 at DLEN 256). So with realistic L2 latency the ray-tracing goal needs DLEN 512 and longer L1 lines, not only the first build; DLEN stays a parameter in RTL step 1. The measured ray-tracing configuration will state N_MISS 8.

  326. 00:49

    ISA extension roadmap (research agent, report build/research/isa-roadmap.md, ignored, read-only; nothing run). RVA23U64 has 33 mandatory extensions on RV64I: M and B are done, and F/D, Zicsr (fcsr only) and V (a Zve64f subset) are in progress. Mandatory but missing from the user's table: Zimop, Zcmop, Zihintpause, Zihintntl, Zic64b, Ziccif, Ziccrse, Ziccamoa, Zicclsm, Za64rs and Supm. Findings: the hint extensions already run as no-ops and need only directed tests. Zimop must write 0 to rd, where today it traps. Multiply value prediction (since 0.11.1) likely conflicts with Zkt. C would give about no clock gain on the out-of-order core. The timer needs a PMA step first. Proposed order: one MINOR per step, starting 0.18.0 V, then Zicond, Zfa, Zimop and the hints, then an M-mode foundation before A, counters, C and full V. Open decisions for the user: Zkt against value prediction, whether A goes before M-mode, and Berkeley TestFloat.

A host binary64 check of fp_ref_core gave 200,000 vectors with 0 mismatches. Findings, with decisions: (1) FP register-file writeback ports are not arbitrated per clock: accepted as a behavioural assumption, and the architecture text must say so. (2) The RTL rounding-mode coverage in fp_cases.py is partial: fix before release, with all modes for FMA, FMUL, FSQRT, FDIV and FCVT, plus an FSQRT.S latency chain (assigned to middle). (3) tb_bench and tb_visualizer still log rb_rd[4:0], so FP writes look like x writes: fix before release (middle).   331. 01:00

    RPCS3 SIMD research (research agent; report build/research/rpcs3-simd.md, ignored; read on the web at RPCS3 commit 3bfac607760d39dcdbe77eb137ab6dbb723b3b36, GPL-2.0, no code copied; rpcs3.net returned 403). Findings: SPU and VMX values stay 128-bit even on AVX-512 hosts, so AVX-512's gain comes from instructions (two-source byte permutes, vpternlog, rotates, vrangeps and vfixupimm clamps, VNNI, GFNI as a bit-gather), not width. Gather, compress, conflict detection and F16C are not used in the parts read. The one end-to-end number (issue 16733, Ryzen 9800X3D) is AVX-512 target 72 fps against AVX2 56 fps on LLVM 16, 67 against 59 on LLVM 19. Mapping, as the agent's judgement: SHUFB in about 6-8 RVV ops with vrgather. Missing from RVV-CONTRACT: Zvbb, fixed point with vxsat, widening, vadc, vfrec7 and a 3-input logic op. The top requirements are integer ALU on every vector pipe (the current plan has it on VP2 only), a 1-per-clock e8 vrgather, and 2-3 clock vector-to-scalar moves. Decision (coordinator): step 1 is unchanged. The SIMD engineer will score FMA-plus-integer on VP0 and VP1 with ALU-plus-permute on VP2 for the out-of-order steps. Zvbb and fixed point become later MINORs.

    Stand-in adversarial review of SIMD step 1 (Claude, read-only, standing in while Codex is quota-limited; candidate cand.sv sha256 0126bb32 against the released core 8d0357b2). No RTL-versus-golden divergence found. Findings, all accepted and assigned to the SIMD engineer before 0.18.0. (1) vset{i}vl{i} leaves vstart unchanged, but RVV 1.0 §3.7 says every vector instruction including vset resets it; the RTL, vec_ref and RVV-CONTRACT.md share the error, so the differential test can't see it. (2) The contract's "Zve64f" label is unsupported: fixed point, segments and vfrec7 are excluded, and step 1 makes contract-legal integer, mask, permute and indexed instructions cause 2. Remove it and list what step 1 executes. (3) The tests compare only against vec_ref, written alongside the RTL; add rv_iss_fp --vector as a second model. (4) A same-edge ordering race in tb_v.py's W queue; run under Icarus too. (5) The candidate's header line says 0.14.8. (6) Untested combinations: L0, LD_LAT 2 or more, N_MISS above 1 at VLEN 128/512, masked-off faulting elements, ff with vstart above 0, FP at LMUL 4/8. Checked correct: serialisation into an empty ROB, no VP, fusion or folding on vector entries, store write side effects, fault vstart and tval, vtype vill rules, group and mask overlap checks, FP operand order and NaN boxing. V-off identity was confirmed by an ifdef strip apart from the header line.

Line fills plus a stream prefetcher matter more than MACs.

  360. 01:55

    BitNet / ternary decision notes (coordinator, on build/research/bitnet-ternary.md). Decode of the shipped BitNet b1.58 2B4T is about 1.18 GB per token. On our SoC it is bound by memory-level parallelism, not by the ISA: 4 misses in flight on 8 B beats, with scalar maps already faster than memory feeds them. Widening integer vector ops and integer ALUs on every vector pipe help prefill and L2-resident weights. Conflict to resolve: this report estimates DRAM streaming at about 0.52 B/clk (about 1.8 tok/s at 4 GHz), while the matrix report estimates 0.17 B/clk for a streaming GEMV at the same config. The bound must be measured with the RTL streaming probe (step E1) before either figure is quoted. The two reports also differ on the Zvqdotq / Zvdot4a8i draft status (v0.7 in internal review against v0.0.2 of March 2025); the more recent source must be checked. No custom ternary CPU unit: ternary hardware, if wanted, belongs to the accelerator's GEMV engine (llm-accelerator documents).

  383. 02:56

    CPU catalog and idea list (Sonnet research agent, web only, nothing run; build/research/cpu-catalog/catalog.json and catalog.csv, build/research/cpu-catalog.md, build/research/cpu-ideas.md). 67 entries: 46 tier-1 (Intel, AMD, Apple, Arm, Qualcomm, NVIDIA, Fujitsu, Samsung, RISC-V, IBM) and 21 tier-2 designs and IP blocks. Top ideas: ITTAGE indirect prediction (JS dispatch), FDIP fetch-directed I-prefetch, short-forward-branch predication (note: a predication design was rejected earlier in this project after a cost-charged model showed a loss and a Codex review found critical problems; any revival must address those findings first), a block BTB with a zero-bubble micro-BTB, two taken branches per fetch, a VTAGE/EVES context value predictor, branch resolution from predicted guard values (speculative), a TAGE-SC-L corrector, RRIP/SHiP L2 replacement for LZMA, and a non-scheduling dispatch queue.   395. 02:55

    FFmpeg decisions (coordinator, on build/research/ffmpeg.md; FFmpeg n9.1 = 74e43935, LGPL/GPL, kept out of the repository). Of 647 RISC-V entry points, vector step 1 can run 20 (3%) and the full RVV-CONTRACT 97 (15%). The rest need widening (423), fixed point with vxrm (377), narrowing (155) or segments (105). The step-1 vector unit drains the ROB on every vector instruction and moves one element per clock, so it will likely be slower than scalar C on streaming kernels until the pipelined, beat-wide vector unit lands; that unit is the precondition for any FFmpeg gain. No A or Zicond is needed except for threading. Proposed --benchmark ff (part A, about 14 FFmpeg DSP kernels driven the way checkasm drives them; part B, an H.264 CABAC decode of a generated QCIF clip, FLAC, then dav1d) is noted for later and not started. Integer-vector, widening/narrowing, fixed-point and segment MINORs are confirmed as the vector roadmap after 0.18.0.

  398. 03:08

    Standards review (Sonnet research agent, web only; build/research/standards.md; the paywalled IEEE 754 text was not read, and a few items are marked unverified). Governing documents: IEEE 754-2019 plus RISC-V F/D/Zfh/Zfa for binary16/32/64; RISC-V Zfbfmin/Zvfbfmin/Zvfbfwma for BF16 (not IEEE); OCP OFP8 and MX v1.0; IEEE P3109 is still a draft; the RISC-V V spec for fixed point (vxrm, vxsat); int4/ternary/GGUF packings are de facto only. Metrics: IEC 80000-13 / IEEE 1541 prefixes; Weicker and the 1757 VAX constant for DMIPS; the 7-Zip rating is defined in Bench.cpp and is nominal, not retired instructions; SPEC CPU 2017 reporting rules; SMARTS and SimPoint for sampling. Top gaps: (1) no independent FP oracle, since fracoracle is our own code and Sail/Spike were cited but never run; fix with Berkeley TestFloat/SoftFloat 3e (BSD), which needs the user's approval because it is not on the official-source list. (2) No operation x rounding-mode x boundary matrix. (3) Tininess checked only against our own models. (4) FP-CONTRACT and architecture cite IEEE 754-2008 but use 2019 min/max (wording). (5) The JS geomean has no CI (wording/test). (6) The 7-Zip doc calls the rating MIPS and says 4 MB for 4 MiB (wording). (7) RVV fixed point and (8) Zfa/Zfhmin/BF16 are contract MINORs. (9) KB/KiB mixing and the 'fast runtime' Dhrystone labelling (wording). (10) No SystemVerilog version statement (wording). Plan: test items first, one wording commit with no version bump, then the contract MINORs.

    Prefetch decisions (coordinator, on build/research/prefetch.md). Why there is none: three tries were measured where they couldn't pay off (13:11 I-prefetch on flat memory; 19:16 split-channel I-prefetch, superseded by the I-line plan; 21:25 next-line plus PC-stride, parked because BT4's hash and son loads are random); the streaming benchmarks that need one only arrived at 03:03-03:40. Offline: a 16-line L2 stream prefetcher covers 100% of mm GEMV and ternary DRAM misses at accuracy 1.00; nothing reaches d22 compress misses (stream +2.5%, SMS, Markov and DMP worse or polluting). Surprise: a zero-RTL perfect-L2 stand-in (MEM_LAT 21) gives d22 compress -57.6% (12 pieces), so d22 compress is DRAM-latency bound but not prefetchable. That gap belongs to L2 capacity (docket 6) and to more misses in flight; N_MISS 8 at d22 is the next cheap bound (bandwidth engineer). Docket: D1, the L2 stream prefetcher, refines item 11 (bandwidth engineer; 16 streams x 16 lines, MEM_GAP-charged; expected mm -75 to -84%, T-2B4T -60 to -75%, decompress -8 to -10%); D3 keeps I lines plus next-line, with next-line sent with the demand miss (middle); D4, FDIP, is new as item 12 (front end, after items 7-10); D5, L1D per-PC stride on 64 B lines, is new as item 13, low priority (middle, after dline). Rejected with data: SMS, Markov, DMP (also the GoFetch side channel); Zicbop parked.

  490. 04:21

    RV64GC + privilege + MMU research for Linux (research agent, web only, nothing built or downloaded; build/research/rv64gc-linux.md). Linux needs rv64ima: the kernel's -march starts with rv64ima, C and F/D are kernel options, OpenSBI needs rv64ima_zicsr and emulates AMOs over LR/SC and a missing time CSR, and Sv39 alone suffices. RV64GC matters for distro binaries, V8 and Node, not for booting. Proposed minimal platform 'lab-virt': Spike's memory map, one hart, M/S/U, Sv39 with Svade, CLINT, ns16550 UART, an optional 1-source PLIC, a test device, deterministic time (mtime = instret/100), OpenSBI fw_jump plus Linux and a busybox initramfs. A boot is roughly 315-379M instructions to a shell (third-party count). Estimated from DEVLOG rates: multicycle RTL about 1-2 min, out-of-order about 16-30 min, so out-of-order runs use checkpoints. Staged MINORs, each behind a default-off define: S1 M-mode, traps, mret; S2 FS/VS, counters, PMA plus CLINT, interrupts; S3 A; S4 S/U and delegation; S5 Sv39; S6 SoC devices; E1 Linux on the multicycle RTL; then the pipelined and out-of-order cores; C last. Golden models: rv_iss.c gains a system mode for boot, checkpoints and lockstep; Spike is the privileged authority; Sail arbitrates; reference.py stays user-mode. First milestone M1: rv_iss --sys boots OpenSBI, Linux and busybox to a shell, matches Spike's commit log over a window, and writes full-system checkpoints; about 2-3 weeks of one agent. GPL code stays out of the repository. Departures from isa-roadmap.md: the supervisor track comes before RVA23U64 closure, and the multicycle core goes first. Not started; awaiting the user.

    MLP/MSHR measurements (MLP research agent; private copies under build/research/mlp/; HEAD 4d13324 / 0.17.4; Verilator 5.020, clang 23.1.5 -O2, --jobs 1, each under 300 s, all PASS; cold streamall probe, L2 256 KiB / 20, MEM 350, GAP 8). Per-beat entries waste capacity: 48-67% of entry time waits on a line another entry already requested, and 18-37% of split reads are entry-0 duplicates. DLINE 64 N4: 0.5324; DLINE 64 N8: 1.2378; DLINE 64 N16 (copy): 1.4091 with only 8.7 of 16 entries busy; DLINE 64 N16 with IQ 256 (copy): 2.6285.   498. 04:40

    MLP decisions (coordinator, on build/research/mlp-mshr.md). Little's law at 350-clock DRAM: 8 B/clk needs about 45 per-line entries, 20-32 B/clk 113-180. Shipping cores: Golden Cove 16 L1D fill buffers / 48 L2 queue, Lion Cove 80, Zen 4 24, Neoverse V2 96, Oryon over 50 / over 220, Apple with 128 B lines at about 32 B/clk from one M1 Max core. The SoC model caps at 8 B/clk at any depth (8 B per answer clock, and MEM_GAP 8 is 8 B/clk), so the 20-32 B/clk M6 goal needs a 32-64 B response path and MEM_GAP of 2-3 or less: an assumption change for the user. Steps: S0, N_MISS 8 plus DLINE 64 as the streaming config after 0.18.0 / 0.19.0; S2, measure on mm V1 and T-2B4T; S3 (PATCH, middle), entry 0 adopts an in-flight line instead of re-reading; S4 (PATCH), wake sleeping loads by MSHR index (FPGA area); S5, an L2_MSHR cap in the SoC sized 48-64 for the D1 prefetcher with 4-8 demand slots reserved (bandwidth engineer); S7 (MINOR), 5-bit tags, only with an IQ that doesn't hold sleeping loads. Stores stay no-write-allocate.

  519. 05:03

    Decompress tail is the output CRC pass; possible benchmark-fidelity issue (backend; instrumented SoC/tb copy in mirror l2c/inst, cpu-v0.16.0, same d22 plan; counters only, piece 113 at 6 MiB gives the same 107,432 clocks). In piece 113, 150,003 of 150,001 window retirements are in lzma_crc_update (0xbd2c-0xc214); the counter includes the window-edge instruction. Pieces 116 and 119 also resume there. Its 2,083 L2 line reads all hit in output_buf, with 0 DRAM fills. So the tail is lzma_crc_update(output_buf, 4 MiB + 64 KiB) after LzmaDec_DecodeToDic, one byte per step: at 4 MiB the 4.06 MiB output plus 1.70 MiB packed data don't fit, so a sequential LRU scan misses on every line. Flag, not verified: 7-Zip's Bench.cpp may CRC the output incrementally as it is written, with multi-table or hardware CRC (7zCrc.c). If so, our tail overweights decompress toward L2 capacity. Backend assigned to check the official 7-Zip source and our harness and the laptop reference, then quantify; any change to the deciding benchmark goes to the user.

  527. 05:22

    7-Zip decompress fidelity: our harness departs from 7-Zip (backend; official 7-Zip 26.06 source at d3296e39, the commit the targets doc cites, read-only in build/third-party/7zip). 7-Zip: Bench.cpp CEncoderInfo::Decode (line 1567) writes into a CCrcOutStream (1589, 1697) whose Write (382-390) runs CrcUpdate on each block; CRC is on in every LZMA pass (CheckCrcMode_Always, 1128 and 1919); the timing brackets the decode loop (SetStartTime 2105, Decode 2130, SetFinishTime 2159); LzmaDecoder.cpp writes every 1 MiB _outStep (32, 139-141, 178); 7zCrc.c uses Arm hardware __crc32d where available (this laptop has crc32) and slicing-by-12 otherwise, with no RISC-V hardware path. Ours (lzma_bench.c): decompress_kernel runs one DecodeToDic of the whole output, then a separate byte-table CRC pass at 9 instructions per byte; cpu/lzma-benchmark.md:43 mentions the byte CRC but not that it is a post-pass. The laptop kernel reference runs the same lzma_bench.c, so the kernel-to-kernel comparison is same-algorithm, but the bridge to 7z b absorbs the X925's hardware-CRC cost. CRC share of decompress clocks (cpu-v0.16.0, deciding config): d22 24.4% at 4 MiB L2 and 18.8% at 6 MiB; d12 10.8%; native X925 13.2% (d22) and 16.8% (d12). Pieces 113-119 are 100% CRC. Variant v3 (1 MiB decode steps, slicing-by-12 CRC per step inside the timed region, tables built before timing; backend/crc/mkvar.py): CRCs equal, compare_ok PASS; RTL d12 decompress -7.0% (231,764 to 215,455); native X925 -11.2% (d22) and -13.6% (d12). Estimated if adopted (d22 RTL not yet run): d22 decompress goal about 2.18 to 2.45, ours about 2.32 to 2.8 MIPS/MHz (106% to about 113-116% of goal); d12 goal 3.35 to about 3.88. Most of L2c's decompress gain would vanish. Nothing changed; the benchmark change is the user's decision.

  532. 05:58

    7-Zip CRC fix: a design fix found by the compress pairing check (backend). The first d22 compress pieces on 0.18.0 (pieces 10-11) differed from the goal run by +1.50% and -0.84%. Cause: building the 11 extra slicing tables in lzma_crc_init, before compression, shifted every compress window by +31,858 instructions, so the pieces no longer covered the same windows. It was not code layout. Fix: the tables live in the arena and are built after compression, just before LZMA_MARK_DEC_CALL, untimed in both phases (7-Zip builds them at start-up, also untimed). rv_iss on the rebuilt d22 image: compress kernel 3,535,891,073 instructions, identical to the goal run, starting +12 instructions later (116,225,239 against 116,225,227), so the pairing holds; decompress kernel 310,047,012 instructions against 327,085,245 (-5.2%). Earlier-variant numbers (d12 deciding decompress 229,974 to 220,307, and the first suite run) are superseded; the d22 runs were stopped and their pieces deleted, then everything is re-run on the final code: native re-measure, suite baseline, d12, and d22 interleaved on 0.18.0 and 0.16.0.

A state-count model with coefficients from 18 microbenchmarks gives: default 32-wide / ROB 512 at 30-69-89M LUT6 (snapshots 57%, ROB 33%), 8-wide / ROB 256 at 4.5-12.7M, 4-wide / ROB 128 at 1.4-3.6M; issue select is a sequential chain of 1,084 levels at 64 entries with 4 picks. The 32-wide model as written fits no FPGA (4-20x a VP1902). Recommendation: an ECP5 ULX3S 85F (about $235, price from a search snippet) for the pipelined core now (49%, open flow on this machine); an FPGA-oriented 4-wide out-of-order core estimated at 70-150k LUT6 plus 20-40k for the FPU (Genesys 2 K325T $1,183 or KCU116 $6,495); 8-wide at 250-500k LUT6 (VCU118 VU9P $14,995). Decisions for the user: Vivado (needs an x86 host), no full-size run (hours to days, over 100 GB), whether to commission an FPGA-oriented out-of-order configuration, and whether to turn localparams into parameters and fix the Yosys-incompatible constructs.

  590. 06:51

    C extension and misaligned access research report (Sonnet research agent; build/research/rvc-misaligned.md, raw data in build/research/rvc-misaligned/results.txt). Today in rv64_ooo.sv: no C decoder (a PC with bit 1 set gives cause 0), a misaligned load or store gives cause 4 or 6, and there is no mtvec. RVA23U64 mandates Zca, Zcd, Zcb, Zcmop and Zicclsm. Zama16b is an RVA23 development option, not mandatory. Measured with clang 23.1.5 rv64im with and without c (ISS sizes and byte counts, not cycles): Dhrystone text 3,996 -> 3,034 B (-24.1%), dynamic bytes -28.2%; 7-Zip d12 text 54,704 -> 41,172 B (-24.7%), dynamic bytes -22.1%. 51-56% of 32-bit instructions sit at PC mod 4 = 2, and 3-14% cross a 64 B line. No public 32-wide C decoder was found. Recommendation: misaligned first (M0 models and tests, M1 MINOR in-line beat crossing), then C in several steps, after ITTAGE and IWAYS, because both touch predictor indexing. Coordinator: accepted onto the docket in that order, with nothing started. Open questions go to the user: trap emulation versus hardware for Zicclsm, and whether compressed code must decode at full width.

  591. 06:52

    Counters and RVA23 audit research report (Sonnet research agent; build/research/counters-rva23.md and the notes events.md, audit.md, plan.md; read-only, nothing simulated). rv64_ooo.sv has no counters and only fflags/frm/fcsr (FPU builds). rv_iss --sys has Zicntr, with hpm CSRs reading 0. The trace profile has no mispredict, miss, MSHR or stall data. Hints (Zihintpause, Zihintntl, Zicbop) already execute but are undeclared; pause is a serialising FENCE. Zicond and Zimop are one decode away. Zkt conflicts with value prediction of mul. The isa-roadmap version numbers are stale (it plans 0.19.0 as Zicond). Recommended order, one version each: hints declared and tested; Zicond MINOR; Zimop MINOR; read-only cycle/time/instret with the N trace record MINOR; event bus plus testbench counters, default off; then Zihpm and Sscofpmf after the privilege CSR file. Coordinator decisions: accepted onto the docket in that order. The lockstep cycle value uses the N record. time uses the lab model until the ACLINT step. A ROB drain per rdcycle is acceptable for now. The event bus is a PATCH if it adds no port and changes no architectural behaviour, otherwise a MINOR. Making pause a no-op is a later PATCH. Zkt is decided after the cost of excluding mul from value prediction is measured. None of these is assigned yet, pending the other four research reports.

  592. 06:53

    Full RVV research report (Sonnet research agent; build/research/rvv-complete.md, static scripts in build/research/rvv-complete/). In the RTL, RV64_OOO_V executes vset*, the vector CSRs, ld/st and FP32 arithmetic. Every integer, mask, permute, reduction, indexed and segment instruction, and every FP SEW other than 32, raises cause 2. The contract and vec_ref.py already define the integer, mask, permute, reduction and indexed groups. rv_iss.c has no vector support. Static counts, clang 23.1.5 -O2 -march=rv64gcv on our bench sources: 3,245 vector instructions over 116 mnemonics; only 157 of the 460 functions with vector code would run on step 1. The top blockers are vmv.v.*/vmerge (258 functions), vector-scalar moves (60), extends (46) and the integer ALU (42). RVA23 mandates V, Zvfhmin, Zvbb and Zvkt. Zvkt requires data-independent latency in the integer vector pipes, so there must be no value prediction or early-out there. Recommended order after 2a: 2b (integer ALU, vmv/vmerge, compare, mask, extends, scalar moves); 2c (mul, div, slides, vrgather, vcompress, reductions, with a DIEL timing test from here on); 2d (indexed); then widening, Zvbb, fixed point, segments, FP64, Zvfhmin and the VS state. Coordinator decisions: (a) executing an instruction that used to raise cause 2 changes the ISA the core implements, so each coverage step is a MINOR, even when the contract already defines it, and 2a stays a PATCH; (b) the RTL lockstep oracle for vector work is a vector mode in rv_iss, carried over from build/cpu/fp/rv_iss_fp.c with provenance, with Spike as the independent second oracle, and both go on the docket before 2b; (c) 2b-2d are re-scoped against the real 2a RTL once it lands, and the opcode count is re-run dynamically then.

  598. 07:04

    Atomics and memory-ordering research report (atomics research agent; build/research/atomics-rtl.md, notes in atomics-rtl/). No core executes A: LR, SC and AMOs raise cause 2, as do wrs.nto and cbo.zero; every FENCE form and FENCE.I execute. The out-of-order core is RVWMO-compliant only as the single master: there is no snoop, and load value prediction would break address-dependency order with a second agent. A FENCE holds younger loads, including for fences with no read successor, which are 41% of Linux boot FENCEs. Benchmark images have 0 atomics and 0 fences; the model Linux boot has 0.295% atomics. RVA23U64 mandates A, Ziccrse, Ziccamoa, Za64rs and Zawrs. Zacas, Zabha and Zama16b are development options, and Ztso is not in the profile. Plan: A0 models and tests (no bump); A1 MINOR, LR/SC/AMO at the ROB head in the out-of-order core; A2 PATCH fence decode; A3 Zawrs; A4 PMA and IO; A5 snoop port with a second-agent testbench; A6 speculative ordering; A7 Zabha; A8 Zacas; A9 A in the pipelined core. Coordinator decisions: accepted onto the docket. A0 adds A to reference.py and user-mode rv_iss. Reservations use the exact address until snoops exist, and the core's own ordinary stores do not clear them, as in the current contract. Load value prediction stays, with its contribution to be measured before A5. Ztso is not advertised. Which core gets A first, and the herd7 install, wait for the privilege and MMU reports. Not yet assigned.

  629. 07:45

    S3 v1 blocked by Codex review, v2 in test (middle, hdl/codex-s3-review.txt). (1) High, livelock found by inspection: after an adopted read's error, entry 0 was freed, so younger loads could keep re-reading the line and the oldest load keep adopting, and a persistent fault might never trap. Fix: on an error on the adopted beat, entry 0 drops the adoption and sends its own tag-0 read, keeping the line in flight; the wake happens only on a good delivery. New cases line_fault_adopted_first and line_fault_adopted_last. (2) High, text: with transaction-dependent memory errors, adopting a good beat skips a read that might have failed. Coordinator decision: a PATCH. The 0.19.0 contract already allows transaction-dependent outcomes (a failed beat may succeed later, and another entry's fill can satisfy the oldest load), the guarantee that cause 5 comes only from entry 0's own read is unchanged, and the explicit sentence stays. (3) Medium, test: line_fault_once_adopted could pass without adoption, so tb_core now counts fault_once_late and the test requires at least 1; fusion must fail it. (4) Low: the immediate-delivery path also wakes exact-beat waiters. New core 970eb4ae0e35. v1 ran the default build 16/16 bit-exact to fusion; v2 is rerunning everything.

2026-11-20

20 novembre 2026

setup, review rubric, roadmap and first benchmark

Findings

  1. The local toolchain did not match the docs. The README and quick start say Ubuntu 24.04 under WSL with Icarus is already installed. On this ARM64 Windows workstation WSL 2.7.10 was present but had no distribution. Installed Ubuntu 24.04, Icarus Verilog 12.0 and Python 3.12.3. Windows has Python 3.12.10 (ARM64) and LLVM 23.1.5.
  2. The core passes its own tests. The main regression passed 96 of 96 differential runs plus the reset and RAM-wrapper checks, in about 12 seconds. The historical PicoRV32 smoke test passed in 26 cycles.
  3. GitHub Pages is not enabled for the repository. The Pages API returns 404 and there is no Pages branch or workflow. The project's pages (CPU visualizer and core explainer) are served locally by sim/cpu/serve_visualizer.py.
  4. Building the visualizer lessons rewrites two tracked files. cpu/programs-verification.json and cpu/visualizer-verification.json change on every lesson build, and again when Run simulation is clicked in the viewer. Both are modified and uncommitted; the decision is tracked in TODO.md.
  5. The core cannot yet be called "good" or "performant". A review rubric was written (cpu/review-rubric.md). The author's gate reading at commit 0483d99: external-model check, lint and synthesis, known-issue list and independent review are not met, and no performance gate can be met without synthesis or a board.
  6. V8 is not a usable benchmark. It needs an operating system, an MMU, RV64GC-class hardware and hundreds of megabytes of memory, and would take many hours per billion instructions in RTL simulation. Chosen instead: CoreMark (primary) and Dhrystone (secondary), with the riscv-tests benchmark programs as the next small set.
  7. RISC-V has a ratified vector extension but no ratified matrix extension (as of mid-2026). Integrated (IME) and attached (AME) matrix approaches are recorded as research in the to-do list.
  8. The core has no caches. Main memory is a 16 KiB RAM that answers in one clock, so caches cannot speed up today's simulation. A staged plan (slow memory model, then L1, then L2, measured at each step) is in the to-do list.
  9. Dhrystone runs on the unchanged core: 0.260 DMIPS/MHz in simulation (non-compliant run, rubric evidence class E1). Details below.
  10. Benchmark scores depend heavily on the compiler. The same Dhrystone source scores 0.260 DMIPS/MHz with clang 23.1.5, 0.249 with clang 21.1.6 (the CI runner) and 0.217 with Ubuntu's clang 18.1.3. All three pass every check.
  11. Clocks per instruction follow from the memory timing. The RAM acknowledges one clock after each request, so a fetch takes 2 clocks and execution 1. Most instructions take 3 clocks; loads and stores take 5. Dhrystone averages 3.65; the earlier C demo, with fewer memory operations, averages about 3.05.
  12. The simulator is faster than first estimated. The C demo record implies about 17,000 instructions per second. The Dhrystone timing run retired 307,336 instructions over 1,120,105 clocks in 9.0 seconds: about 34,000 instructions or 124,000 clocks per second, including process start-up.
  13. This LLVM installation has no RISC-V runtime library. Bare-metal programs that multiply or divide need their own helper routines; the benchmark runtime now provides them.
  14. Existing verification records contain local user paths. cpu/verification.json and cpu/c-demo-results.json include absolute paths from the machine that produced them. The new Dhrystone record stores repository-relative paths instead. The older records are unchanged.
  15. The repository check script could not be run locally in this session because the assistant's permission settings blocked it. CI runs it on every push, and it passed for each commit below.
  16. First rubric scorecard: 37.1 of 100, the lowest ("foundation") band, no claims granted. Strongest: traps and reset (R2) and reproducibility (R9), both 62%. Weakest: FPGA implementation (R5) at 0%, performance (R4) at 22% and verification depth (R3) at 25%. It is an automated self-assessment, recomputed on every Dhrystone run from cpu/rubric-evidence.json.
  17. Correction to the rubric's section 11 reading. That reading listed HG1 (scope label) as met, but the scope label has no performance target, which rubric section 2 requires. The evidence file records HG1 as not met until a target is declared. Section 11 is left as written, since it is a dated reading.
  18. The CPU architecture is now versioned: 0.1.0. The unchanged C0 core, called "revision 0" until now, becomes 0.1.0. Before 1.0.0, MINOR changes for any change to the architectural contract and PATCH for any other RTL change. Proposed criteria for 1.0.0: C1 complete, an external-model check (rubric HG3) and a first exact-part synthesis. Rules and history: cpu/architecture.md section 9.
  19. Profile of Dhrystone: fetching is 54.8% of all clocks. Each instruction spends 2 clocks being fetched and 1 executing; loads and stores add 2. The per-instruction clock model reproduces the measured timed region exactly (1,094,103 clocks); the whole-program counts differ by 3 clocks, the final ebreak.
  20. The byte-at-a-time string routines dominate. strcpy and strcmp from sim/cpu/bench/runtime.c execute 47% of all instructions (281 of 600 per run). Software divide is only 5% of clocks (one __divdi3 call per run); multiplication never reaches __muldi3 because the compiler uses shifts. This corrects advice given earlier in the session that hardware multiply and divide would be the biggest gain for Dhrystone: the profile estimates only 1.05x.
  21. 40% of instructions could reuse the previous fetch. 241 of 600 instructions per run are the second half of an 8-byte fetch beat that was just read. Model estimates: a one-entry fetch buffer gives 1.12x to 1.28x (2,188 down to 1,947 or 1,706 clocks per run); an ideal pipeline gives at most 3.65x. These are estimates from the trace, not measured RTL.
  22. Run logs are now split by commit. The two runs previously logged at the end of this file were moved unchanged to devlog/devlog-3110337.md.
  23. The optimization loop took Dhrystone from 2,188.2 to 555.7 clocks per run, 3.94x (0.260 to 1.024 DMIPS/MHz, simulation). Order of gains: instruction fetch first (fetch buffer 1.28x, then a 4 KiB instruction cache 1.73x), then memory waits (early issue 1.24x, same-clock writes 1.11x, a data cache 1.20x), then instruction count (M, 1.08x on the rv64im build).
  24. The loop stopped at about one instruction per clock. On 0.4.0 only 2.6 of 555.7 clocks per run are not instruction execution: two divide steps, 0.6 fetch and 0.1 memory. Lower needs fewer instructions (compiler or runtime, not the core) or more than one instruction per clock (a superscalar redesign, not a refinement).
  25. The gains depend on assumptions not yet tested. Both 4 KiB caches hold all of Dhrystone, and the RAM answers in one clock. The cache lookups use the just-computed next PC and data address in the same clock, which may lower the clock frequency; nothing has been synthesized. A slow-memory model and a first synthesis are the checks that matter next.
  26. Two tooling problems found and fixed during the loop. The visualizer testbench detected writes in a way that stopped working at 0.2.2. The profiler's "issue loads and stores in EXECUTE" estimate overstated the gain for 0.3.0 and 0.4.0; the run logs devlog-90ee1ea.md and devlog-88db627.md still show the old figure and are left as written.
  27. The core contract changed in three MINOR steps. ICACHE_BEATS (0.2.0) and DCACHE_BEATS (0.3.0) are new parameters, every address is now treated as cacheable, and 0.4.0 adds M. Each version has a history row in cpu/architecture.md and a local cpu-vX.Y.Z tag.

The script now snapshots and hashes its source first

  113. about 23:25

    Finding: depth grew 2.6x from 0.3.0 while DMIPS/MHz grew about 2.1x. If clock frequency fell in proportion to depth, absolute performance would not have improved. Three variants of 0.7.0 are being synthesized (as built, multiplier removed, multiplier removed with forwarding and folding off) to find what the deep path is

    output
  114. about 23:25

    Both syntheses contain only flip-flops and LUTs: no latches (part of rubric gate HG7; a dedicated lint run is still missing). Size note: up to three instructions per clock needs six register-file read ports and three write ports, which an FPGA builds from flip-flops and wide multiplexers rather than LUT RAM; LUTs grew from 6,309 to 33,046

    git
  118. about 23:35

    Variant v070-nomul-nofwd (0.7.0 with the multiplier removed, and with folding (control0) and slot-1-to-slot-2 forwarding (slot0_forward) forced off): 40 LUT levels, 28,149 LUTs, 4,483 flip-flops, 442.4 s. Its longest path runs from the register file through about 20 single-LUT steps in a row, the pattern of a 64-bit adder carry built from generic LUTs. A generic LUT mapping has no carry chain, so LUT levels overstate adder delay compared with an FPGA, which has dedicated carry logic

    output
  119. 23:35:50

    nextpnr-ecp5 0.6-3build5 (arm64) installed in Ubuntu with apt-get install nextpnr-ecp5, to get post-route clock estimates on a real FPGA family (Lattice ECP5) instead of LUT levels. New script sim/cpu/synth_fmax.py (Yosys synth_ecp5, then nextpnr out of context, 4-line caches); first runs on 0.3.0, 0.7.0 and the 0.7.1 candidate started

    apt
  120. 23:49

    Variant results (same flow, 4-line caches, run three at a time): 0.7.0 as built 80 LUT levels, 37,991 LUTs, 4,473 flip-flops, 1,266.6 s; multiplier removed 54 levels, 30,188 LUTs, 1,199.3 s; multiplier removed with folding and forwarding off 40 levels, 28,149 LUTs. The multiplier (a 65x65 product after register read and decode in the same clock) accounts for 26 levels and folding plus slot-1-to-slot-2 forwarding for 14 more. (The 0.7.0 RTL gives 80 levels against the 78 of the 0.6.0 run; ABC results vary a little with the design)

    output
  121. 23:50

    Candidate 0.7.1 (registered multiplier) depth: 52 LUT levels against 80 for 0.7.0, 40,187 LUTs (+2,196, from holding the 130-bit product's operands and selecting its result), 4,606 flip-flops (+133), 1,252.4 s. The deepest path no longer goes through the multiplier: it runs from the register file to a register-file write (the issue and forwarding logic). Adopted as 0.7.1 (PATCH: a multiply takes two clocks and no longer pairs; the ISA and ports do not change). Dhrystone does not multiply at run time, so its clocks do not change (256.8, unlogged run at 23:28 to 23:32)

Critical path 44.5 ns, of which 9.2 ns logic and 35.3 ns routing: instruction register, load address, data-cache hit, result select, register-file write. nextpnr exits 1 when the target is missed, so the script reported "nextpnr exit 1"; the report was corrected from the log and the script now passes --timing-allow-fail and records the critical path

Art and science over time. Every figure for this processor comes from simulation of the design in the repository; nothing here is a hardware measurement of it. The one native figure, a laptop's Cortex-X925 running the scaled 7-Zip kernel, is a point of comparison and is labelled as such. The drawings are generated from the architecture history, not photographs of silicon.

Source code and full record: github.com/monstercameron/RISC-V-FPGA-CPU-Research.
